No, not really. I wasn't really a squirmy kid. Like I said before, I was mostly just sitting there, zoning out. I remember in third grade, Mrs. Gable's class... she had this giant fish tank in the back of the room. It had these neon tetras and this one really aggressive angelfish that kept biting the others. No, not really. I never had that feeling of like, a motor running inside me or whatever. I was perfectly fine just sitting there, not moving much. Honestly, I've always been more of a low-energy kind of guy. Even now, my ideal weekend is just crashing on the couch and binge-watching something. I started rewatching Breaking Bad last week. It's crazy how different that show feels when you watch it a second time. Like, you pick up on all these little details they planted in the first season. I think Vince Gilligan is a genius. I tried to get my girlfriend to watch it with me, but she's not really into the violent stuff. She prefers those reality baking shows. We watched The Great British Baking Show, and I swear, every time we watch it I end up craving a croissant or a tart or something. I actually tried making sourdough bread during the pandemic, like everyone else did. My starter died after two weeks though. It smelled like old gym socks, which I guess means it went bad? I don't know, baking is too much of a science for me. You have to measure everything so precisely. If you're off by a little bit of flour, the whole thing is ruined. 

But yeah, hyperactive? No. I was the kid who would just sit still and stare at the wall.

I had this one folder, a blue one with Sonic the Hedgehog on it—man, I loved that game. I used to play it on my Sega Genesis for hours on weekends. My mom would try to get me to go outside, but I was obsessed with beating the Chemical Plant Zone. That level is so hard, the water rises so fast... \n\nBut yeah, the teachers. They said I missed instructions a lot and always needed reminders to finish my classwork. I guess I was just always kind of spaced out.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I could sit in one spot for hours, honestly. Man, that took me forever to put together. We actually took it out to the creek behind our house to see if it would float, but it instantly tipped over and we lost like half the little plastic cannons in the mud. My mom was so mad because it was super expensive... \n\nBut yeah, I wasn't really a bouncy kid. I wasn't running around the classroom or anything like that. I was pretty quiet, just... staring out the window, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often act without thinking?\n\nPatient: Not really. I was actually kind of the opposite, I think. I'd overthink things before doing them, mostly. Like, if my friends were jumping off the high dive at the public pool... we used to go to the one over on 4th street. They actually closed that place down a few years ago because of some city budget thing, which really sucks because they had the best snack bar. They sold those Choco Tacos? I used to get one every time my dad gave me a couple bucks... \n\nWait, what did you ask? Oh, acting without thinking. Yeah, no, I was usually the kid standing at the edge of the diving board for like ten minutes just staring at the water until the lifeguard yelled at me to either jump or climb back down the ladder. So, no, I didn't really just do stuff out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Did you have these attention problems in more than one place, like both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, my mom was always getting on my case about my room being a total disaster zone. I could never keep it clean. She'd tell me to go clean it, and I'd go in there and find some old comic book under my bed and just start reading that instead. I had this huge collection of X-Men comics. The ones from the 90s, you know? With the Jim Lee art. Man, those were the best. I actually still have them in a box somewhere in my apartment. I should really get those graded, I think some of them might be worth a little bit of money now. I wasn't really a squirmy kid. Like I said before, I was mostly just sitting there, zoning out. I remember in third grade, Mrs. Gable's class... she had this giant fish tank in the back of the room. It had these neon tetras and this one really aggressive angelfish that kept biting the others. I used to just sit at my desk and watch that angelfish for what felt like hours. I actually wanted to get a fish tank for my apartment recently, but my landlord is super strict about pets. Even fish, which is ridiculous. What's a fish going to do, ruin the carpet? I mean, I guess if the tank broke... water damage is no joke. I process insurance claims for a living, so I see water damage claims all the time. People leave their sinks running or a pipe bursts while they're on vacation in Florida... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Being restless. No, I could stay in my seat fine. I just wasn't really paying attention to the teacher while I was in it.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often interrupting other people or blurting out answers before a question was finished?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I was pretty quiet, honestly. Blurting things out wasn't really my style. I was usually too busy in my own head to even realize a question was being asked, let alone try to answer it first. My buddy Mark, though, he was terrible about that. We were in Boy Scouts together—Troop 412. We used to go camping up near Bear Lake every summer. The mosquitoes up there were absolutely brutal. I remember one year, I forgot my bug spray and I had like thirty bites just on my left leg. I tried putting calamine lotion on them, but that stuff never really works, does it? It just gets all crusty and pink and flakes off on your sheets. My mom was so mad because I got it all over my sleeping bag, and you can't just throw a down sleeping bag in the regular wash, you have to use those special tennis balls in the dryer... \n\nWait, sorry. Interrupting people. Yeah, no, I didn't really do that. I was more likely to not say anything at all.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often physically hyperactive, like feeling driven to move around or being unable to stay still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I never had that feeling of like, a motor running inside me or whatever. I was perfectly fine just sitting there, not moving much. Honestly, I've always been more of a low-energy kind of guy. Even now, my ideal weekend is just crashing on the couch and binge-watching something. I started rewatching Breaking Bad last week. It's crazy how different that show feels when you watch it a second time. Like, you pick up on all these little details they planted in the first season. I think Vince Gilligan is a genius. I tried to get my girlfriend to watch it with me, but she's not really into the violent stuff. She prefers those reality baking shows. We watched The Great British Baking Show, and I swear, every time we watch it I end up craving a croissant or a tart or something. I actually tried making sourdough bread during the pandemic, like everyone else did. My starter died after two weeks though. It smelled like old gym socks, which I guess means it went bad? I don't know, baking is too much of a science for me. You have to measure everything so precisely. If you're off by a little bit of flour, the whole thing is ruined. \n\nBut yeah, hyperactive? No. I was the kid who would just sit still and stare at the wall.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of being unable to sit still or being physically hyperactive as an adult, too?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I said, I've never been the hyper type. Even now at work, I sit at my desk for like eight hours a day processing claims and I'm fine just sitting there. Well, I mean, my back isn't fine. I actually had to buy one of those lumbar support pillows off Amazon because those office chairs are just terrible. You'd think a big insurance company could afford decent chairs for their employees, right? But no, it's all about the bottom line. My buddy Dave—he works in IT on the fourth floor—he actually managed to get them to buy him a standing desk. He claimed he had some ergonomic doctor's note or something. I tried standing at his desk once, and honestly, my feet started hurting after like twenty minutes. I don't get the hype. Plus, I feel like if I was standing all day, I'd just want to lean on something. I saw this thing online the other day, it's like a leaning stool? It looks so uncomfortable, like half a bicycle seat... \n\nAnyway, no, I don't feel driven to move around or anything like that. I was more likely to not say anything at all.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often physically hyperactive, like feeling driven to move around or being unable to stay still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I never had that feeling of like, a motor running inside me or whatever. I was perfectly fine just sitting there, not moving much. Honestly, I've always been more of a low-energy kind of guy. Even now, my ideal weekend is just crashing on the couch and binge-watching something. I started rewatching Breaking Bad last week. It's crazy how different that show feels when you watch it a second time. Like, you pick up on all these little details they planted in the first season. I think Vince Gilligan is a genius. I tried to get my girlfriend to watch it with me, but she's not really into the violent stuff. She prefers those reality baking shows. We watched The Great British Baking Show, and I swear, every time we watch it I end up craving a croissant or a tart or something. I actually tried making sourdough bread during the pandemic, like everyone else did. My starter died after two weeks though. It smelled like old gym socks, which I guess means it went bad? I don't know, baking is too much of a science for me. You have to measure everything so precisely. If you're off by a little bit of flour, the whole thing is ruined. \n\nBut yeah, hyperactive? No. I was the kid who would just sit still and stare at the wall.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of being unable to sit still or being physically hyperactive as an adult, too?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I said, I've never been the hyper type. Even now at work, I sit at my desk for like eight hours a day processing claims and I'm fine just sitting there. Well, I mean, my back isn't fine. I actually had to buy one of those lumbar support pillows off Amazon because those office chairs are just terrible. You'd think a big insurance company could afford decent chairs for their employees, right? But no, it's all about the bottom line. My buddy Dave—he works in IT on the fourth floor—he actually managed to get them to buy him a standing desk. He claimed he had some ergonomic doctor's note or something. I tried standing at his desk once, and honestly, my feet started hurting after like twenty minutes. I don't get the hype. Plus, I feel like if I was standing all day, I'd just want to lean on something. I saw this thing online the other day, it's like a leaning stool? It looks so uncomfortable, like half a bicycle seat... \n\nAnyway, no, I don't feel driven to move around or anything like that. I'm pretty stationary.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you currently find yourself interrupting other people or blurting things out before they finish speaking?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / insurance_claims_or_policy_processing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't really do that either. I'm usually the one getting interrupted, honestly. I'll be in the middle of explaining a claim to a supervisor and they just talk right over me. It's super frustrating, especially when it's a complicated policy. We have this one underwriter, Sarah, and she is the absolute worst about it. She acts like she knows exactly what you're going to say before you even finish your sentence. And half the time she's wrong! Like last Tuesday, I was trying to tell her about this auto claim where the guy hit a deer, but the deer actually survived and ran off, so there was no carcass for proof. She kept cutting me off saying it was a comprehensive claim, which, yes, I know that, but the issue was the police report didn't match the damage. The bumper was completely caved in on the passenger side, but he said the deer jumped from the left. It just didn't make sense. I actually saw a deer in my neighborhood the other night, which is weird because I live in an apartment complex right off the highway. It was just standing by the dumpsters, staring at me. I tried to take a picture with my phone, but the flash went off and it bolted into the trees. My phone camera is so annoying, I can never remember how to turn the flash off permanently. Every time there's an update, it resets all my settings. Apple really needs to fix that. 

But yeah, blurting things out? No, I'm usually just trying to get a word in edgewise with these people.

No, I wasn't really like that as a kid either. I was always pretty quiet, just doing my own thing. Like I said, I'd just sit and stare at the wall or play with my Legos for hours. I loved Legos. I had this massive pirate ship set that took me like three days to build. It was the Black Seas Barracuda, I think? But no, I wasn't running around all the time or anything like that when I was little. I was definitely one of the calmer kids.

Like, I know I’m supposed to stay put and finish my claim reviews, but I just... I end up wandering off. \n\nActually, they just moved our desks around last month into this open-plan setup. Management said it's supposed to foster \"synergy\" or whatever, but really it just means I can hear my coworker, Greg, chewing his almonds all morning. He eats them one by one, it's so loud. I used to be over by the window, which was nice because I could watch the traffic, but now I'm right next to the breakroom. They got this new espresso machine in there that makes this crazy hissing noise every time someone uses the frother... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Right, sorry. Yeah, I just can't seem to stay in my seat to get my claims done.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar trouble staying seated or paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... looking back, this isn't exactly brand new for me. I remember in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I was, like, a bright kid, but I was always losing my worksheets. Like, constantly. I had this one folder, a blue one with Sonic the Hedgehog on it—man, I loved that game. I used to play it on my Sega Genesis for hours on weekends. My mom would try to get me to go outside, but I was obsessed with beating the Chemical Plant Zone. That level is so hard, the water rises so fast... \n\nBut yeah, the teachers. They said I missed instructions a lot and always needed reminders to finish my classwork. I guess I was just always kind of spaced out.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I could sit in one spot for hours, honestly. Especially if I was drawing or building something. Like, I used to build these huge Lego sets. My uncle got me this massive pirate ship for my birthday one year, the one with the real cloth sails? Man, that took me forever to put together. We actually took it out to the creek behind our house to see if it would float, but it instantly tipped over and we lost like half the little plastic cannons in the mud. My mom was so mad because it was super expensive... \n\nBut yeah, I wasn't really a bouncy kid. I wasn't running around the classroom or anything like that. I was pretty quiet, just... staring out the window, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often act without thinking?\n\nPatient: Not really. I was actually kind of the opposite, I think. I'd overthink things before doing them, mostly. Like, if my friends were jumping off the high dive at the public pool... we used to go to the one over on 4th street. They actually closed that place down a few years ago because of some city budget thing, which really sucks because they had the best snack bar. They sold those Choco Tacos? Man, I heard they discontinued those recently, which is insane to me. They were the best ice cream treat hands down. I used to get one every time my dad gave me a couple bucks... \n\nWait, what did you ask? Oh, acting without thinking. Yeah, no, I was usually the kid standing at the edge of the diving board for like ten minutes just staring at the water until the lifeguard yelled at me to either jump or climb back down the ladder. So, no, I didn't really just do stuff out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Did you have these attention problems in more than one place, like both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, my mom was always getting on my case about my room being a total disaster zone. I could never keep it clean. She'd tell me to go clean it, and I'd go in there and find some old comic book under my bed and just start reading that instead. I had this huge collection of X-Men comics. The ones from the 90s, you know? With the Jim Lee art. Man, those were the best. I actually still have them in a box somewhere in my apartment. I should really get those graded, I think some of them might be worth a little bit of money now. I saw a guy on YouTube the other day who found a pristine copy of X-Men #1 at a garage sale for like fifty cents, which is just crazy... \n\nBut yeah, home, school... it was pretty much the same deal everywhere. Just kind of floating through, not really focusing on what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often restless or unable to stay seated?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wasn't really a squirmy kid. Like I said before, I was mostly just sitting there, zoning out. I remember in third grade, Mrs. Gable's class... she had this giant fish tank in the back of the room. It had these neon tetras and this one really aggressive angelfish that kept biting the others. I used to just sit at my desk and watch that angelfish for what felt like hours. I actually wanted to get a fish tank for my apartment recently, but my landlord is super strict about pets. Even fish, which is ridiculous. What's a fish going to do, ruin the carpet? I mean, I guess if the tank broke... water damage is no joke. I process insurance claims for a living, so I see water damage claims all the time. I just wasn't really paying attention to the teacher while I was in it.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often interrupting other people or blurting out answers before a question was finished?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I was pretty quiet, honestly. Blurting things out wasn't really my style. I was usually too busy in my own head to even realize a question was being asked, let alone try to answer it first. My buddy Mark, though, he was terrible about that. We were in Boy Scouts together—Troop 412. We used to go camping up near Bear Lake every summer. The mosquitoes up there were absolutely brutal. I remember one year, I forgot my bug spray and I had like thirty bites just on my left leg. I tried putting calamine lotion on them, but that stuff never really works, does it? It just gets all crusty and pink and flakes off on your sheets. My mom was so mad because I got it all over my sleeping bag, and you can't just throw a down sleeping bag in the regular wash, you have to use those special tennis balls in the dryer... \n\nWait, sorry. Interrupting people. Yeah, no, I didn't really do that. I was more likely to not say anything at all.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often physically hyperactive, like feeling driven to move around or being unable to stay still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I never had that feeling of like, a motor running inside me or whatever. I was perfectly fine just sitting there, not moving much. Honestly, I've always been more of a low-energy kind of guy. Even now, my ideal weekend is just crashing on the couch and binge-watching something. I started rewatching Breaking Bad last week. It's crazy how different that show feels when you watch it a second time. Like, you pick up on all these little details they planted in the first season. I think Vince Gilligan is a genius. I tried to get my girlfriend to watch it with me, but she's not really into the violent stuff. She prefers those reality baking shows. We watched The Great British Baking Show, and I swear, every time we watch it I end up craving a croissant or a tart or something. I actually tried making sourdough bread during the pandemic, like everyone else did. My starter died after two weeks though. It smelled like old gym socks, which I guess means it went bad? I don't know, baking is too much of a science for me. You have to measure everything so precisely. If you're off by a little bit of flour, the whole thing is ruined. \n\nBut yeah, hyperactive? No. I was the kid who would just sit still and stare at the wall.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of being unable to sit still or being physically hyperactive as an adult, too?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I said, I've never been the hyper type. Even now at work, I sit at my desk for like eight hours a day processing claims and I'm fine just sitting there. Well, I mean, my back isn't fine. I actually had to buy one of those lumbar support pillows off Amazon because those office chairs are just terrible. You'd think a big insurance company could afford decent chairs for their employees, right? But no, it's all about the bottom line. My buddy Dave—he works in IT on the fourth floor—he actually managed to get them to buy him a standing desk. He claimed he had some ergonomic doctor's note or something. I tried standing at his desk once, and honestly, my feet started hurting after like twenty minutes. I don't get the hype. Plus, I feel like if I was standing all day, I'd just want to lean on something. I saw this thing online the other day, it's like a leaning stool? It looks so uncomfortable, like half a bicycle seat... \n\nAnyway, no, I don't feel driven to move around or anything like that. I'm pretty stationary.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently find yourself interrupting other people or blurting things out before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really do that either. I'm usually the one getting interrupted, honestly. I'll be in the middle of explaining a claim to a supervisor and they just talk right over me. It's super frustrating, especially when it's a complicated policy. We have this one underwriter, Sarah, and she is the absolute worst about it. She acts like she knows exactly what you're going to say before you even finish your sentence. And half the time she's wrong! Like last Tuesday, I was trying to tell her about this auto claim where the guy hit a deer, but the deer actually survived and ran off, so there was no carcass for proof. She kept cutting me off saying it was a comprehensive claim, which, yes, I know that, but the issue was the police report didn't match the damage. The bumper was completely caved in on the passenger side, but he said the deer jumped from the left. It just didn't make sense. I actually saw a deer in my neighborhood the other night, which is weird because I live in an apartment complex right off the highway. It was just standing by the dumpsters, staring at me. I tried to take a picture with my phone, but the flash went off and it bolted into the trees. My phone camera is so annoying, I can never remember how to turn the flash off permanently. Every time there's an update, it resets all my settings. Apple really needs to fix that. \n\nBut yeah, blurting things out? No, I'm usually just trying to get a word in edgewise with these people.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often physically hyperactive or always on the go compared with other kids?\n\nPatient: No, I wasn't really like that as a kid either. Yeah, I just can't seem to stay in my seat to get my claims done.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar trouble staying seated or paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... looking back, this isn't exactly brand new for me. I remember in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I was, like, a bright kid, but I was always losing my worksheets. Like, constantly. I had this one folder, a blue one with Sonic the Hedgehog on it—man, I loved that game. I used to play it on my Sega Genesis for hours on weekends. My mom would try to get me to go outside, but I was obsessed with beating the Chemical Plant Zone. That level is so hard, the water rises so fast... \n\nBut yeah, the teachers. They said I missed instructions a lot and always needed reminders to finish my classwork. I guess I was just always kind of spaced out.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I could sit in one spot for hours, honestly. Especially if I was drawing or building something. Like, I used to build these huge Lego sets. My uncle got me this massive pirate ship for my birthday one year, the one with the real cloth sails? Man, that took me forever to put together. We actually took it out to the creek behind our house to see if it would float, but it instantly tipped over and we lost like half the little plastic cannons in the mud. My mom was so mad because it was super expensive... \n\nBut yeah, I wasn't really a bouncy kid. I wasn't running around the classroom or anything like that. I was pretty quiet, just... staring out the window, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often act without thinking?\n\nPatient: Not really. I was actually kind of the opposite, I think. I'd overthink things before doing them, mostly. Like, if my friends were jumping off the high dive at the public pool... we used to go to the one over on 4th street. They actually closed that place down a few years ago because of some city budget thing, which really sucks because they had the best snack bar. They sold those Choco Tacos? Man, I heard they discontinued those recently, which is insane to me. They were the best ice cream treat hands down. I used to get one every time my dad gave me a couple bucks... \n\nWait, what did you ask? Oh, acting without thinking. Yeah, no, I was usually the kid standing at the edge of the diving board for like ten minutes just staring at the water until the lifeguard yelled at me to either jump or climb back down the ladder. So, no, I didn't really just do stuff out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Did you have these attention problems in more than one place, like both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, my mom was always getting on my case about my room being a total disaster zone. I could never keep it clean. She'd tell me to go clean it, and I'd go in there and find some old comic book under my bed and just start reading that instead. I had this huge collection of X-Men comics. The ones from the 90s, you know? With the Jim Lee art. Man, those were the best. I actually still have them in a box somewhere in my apartment. I should really get those graded, I think some of them might be worth a little bit of money now. I saw a guy on YouTube the other day who found a pristine copy of X-Men #1 at a garage sale for like fifty cents, which is just crazy... \n\nBut yeah, home, school... it was pretty much the same deal everywhere. Just kind of floating through, not really focusing on what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often restless or unable to stay seated?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wasn't really a squirmy kid. Like I said before, I was mostly just sitting there, zoning out. I remember in third grade, Mrs. Gable's class... she had this giant fish tank in the back of the room. It had these neon tetras and this one really aggressive angelfish that kept biting the others. I used to just sit at my desk and watch that angelfish for what felt like hours. I actually wanted to get a fish tank for my apartment recently, but my landlord is super strict about pets. Even fish, which is ridiculous. What's a fish going to do, ruin the carpet? I mean, I guess if the tank broke... water damage is no joke. I process insurance claims for a living, so I see water damage claims all the time. People leave their sinks running or a pipe bursts while they're on vacation in Florida... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Being restless. No, I could stay in my seat fine. I just wasn't really paying attention to the teacher while I was in it.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often interrupting other people or blurting out answers before a question was finished?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I was pretty quiet, honestly. Blurting things out wasn't really my style. I was usually too busy in my own head to even realize a question was being asked, let alone try to answer it first. My buddy Mark, though, he was terrible about that. We were in Boy Scouts together—Troop 412. We used to go camping up near Bear Lake every summer. The mosquitoes up there were absolutely brutal. I remember one year, I forgot my bug spray and I had like thirty bites just on my left leg. I tried putting calamine lotion on them, but that stuff never really works, does it? It just gets all crusty and pink and flakes off on your sheets. My mom was so mad because I got it all over my sleeping bag, and you can't just throw a down sleeping bag in the regular wash, you have to use those special tennis balls in the dryer... \n\nWait, sorry. Interrupting people. Yeah, no, I didn't really do that. I was more likely to not say anything at all.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often physically hyperactive, like feeling driven to move around or being unable to stay still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I never had that feeling of like, a motor running inside me or whatever. I was perfectly fine just sitting there, not moving much. Honestly, I've always been more of a low-energy kind of guy. Even now, my ideal weekend is just crashing on the couch and binge-watching something. I started rewatching Breaking Bad last week. It's crazy how different that show feels when you watch it a second time. Like, you pick up on all these little details they planted in the first season. I think Vince Gilligan is a genius. I tried to get my girlfriend to watch it with me, but she's not really into the violent stuff. She prefers those reality baking shows. We watched The Great British Baking Show, and I swear, every time we watch it I end up craving a croissant or a tart or something. I actually tried making sourdough bread during the pandemic, like everyone else did. My starter died after two weeks though. It smelled like old gym socks, which I guess means it went bad? I don't know, baking is too much of a science for me. You have to measure everything so precisely. If you're off by a little bit of flour, the whole thing is ruined. \n\nBut yeah, hyperactive? No. I was the kid who would just sit still and stare at the wall.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of being unable to sit still or being physically hyperactive as an adult, too?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I said, I've never been the hyper type. Even now at work, I sit at my desk for like eight hours a day processing claims and I'm fine just sitting there. Well, I mean, my back isn't fine. I actually had to buy one of those lumbar support pillows off Amazon because those office chairs are just terrible. You'd think a big insurance company could afford decent chairs for their employees, right? But no, it's all about the bottom line. My buddy Dave—he works in IT on the fourth floor—he actually managed to get them to buy him a standing desk. He claimed he had some ergonomic doctor's note or something. I tried standing at his desk once, and honestly, my feet started hurting after like twenty minutes. I don't get the hype. Plus, I feel like if I was standing all day, I'd just want to lean on something. I saw this thing online the other day, it's like a leaning stool? It looks so uncomfortable, like half a bicycle seat... \n\nAnyway, no, I don't feel driven to move around or anything like that. I'm pretty stationary.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently find yourself interrupting other people or blurting things out before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really do that either. I'm usually the one getting interrupted, honestly. I'll be in the middle of explaining a claim to a supervisor and they just talk right over me. It's super frustrating, especially when it's a complicated policy. We have this one underwriter, Sarah, and she is the absolute worst about it. She acts like she knows exactly what you're going to say before you even finish your sentence. And half the time she's wrong! Like last Tuesday, I was trying to tell her about this auto claim where the guy hit a deer, but the deer actually survived and ran off, so there was no carcass for proof. She kept cutting me off saying it was a comprehensive claim, which, yes, I know that, but the issue was the police report didn't match the damage. The bumper was completely caved in on the passenger side, but he said the deer jumped from the left. It just didn't make sense. I actually saw a deer in my neighborhood the other night, which is weird because I live in an apartment complex right off the highway. It was just standing by the dumpsters, staring at me. I tried to take a picture with my phone, but the flash went off and it bolted into the trees. My phone camera is so annoying, I can never remember how to turn the flash off permanently. Every time there's an update, it resets all my settings. Apple really needs to fix that. \n\nBut yeah, blurting things out? No, I'm usually just trying to get a word in edgewise with these people.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often physically hyperactive or always on the go compared with other kids?\n\nPatient: No, I wasn't really like that as a kid either. I was always pretty quiet, just doing my own thing. Like I said, I'd just sit and stare at the wall or play with my Legos for hours. I loved Legos. I had this massive pirate ship set that took me like three days to build. It was the Black Seas Barracuda, I think? It had these real cloth sails and little cannons that actually fired these tiny plastic cannonballs. I ended up losing most of the cannonballs under the couch, though. My mom used to get so mad when she'd vacuum and hear them rattling up the hose. Our vacuum was this ancient Hoover that weighed like fifty pounds. It sounded like a jet engine taking off in the living room. I swear, they don't make appliances like they used to. My microwave broke last month, just completely stopped heating things up. The turntable spun, the light came on, but the food was ice cold. I looked up how to fix it on YouTube, but all the comments were like, \"Don't touch the capacitor or you'll electrocute yourself,\" so I just threw it out and bought a new one at Target. \n\nBut no, I wasn't running around all the time or anything like that when I was little. I was definitely one of the calmer kids.\n\nDoctor: Were you often physically hyperactive as a child?\n\nPatient: No, I think you just asked me that, didn't you? Like I was saying, I wasn't really hyper. I just sat around a lot. Actually, speaking of sitting around, my mom has this old photo album she brought out last Thanksgiving. It's one of those ones with the sticky pages and the clear plastic you peel back? Those things are terrible for preserving photos, by the way. The acid in the glue turns everything yellow. My aunt is a scrapbooking fanatic, and she gave my mom a whole lecture about archival-quality paper. My aunt has an entire room in her house just for scrapbooking. She calls it her \"craft sanctuary.\" It's full of all these little punches and stamps and expensive markers. I don't know how she affords it all, honestly. She works part-time at a florist, so it's not like she's rolling in dough. But I guess everyone needs a hobby. \n\nBut yeah, like I said, I was a pretty calm kid. Not hyperactive.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. She acts like she knows exactly what you're going to say before you even finish your sentence. And half the time she's wrong! Like last Tuesday, I was trying to tell her about this auto claim where the guy hit a deer, but the deer actually survived and ran off, so there was no carcass for proof. She kept cutting me off saying it was a comprehensive claim, which, yes, I know that, but the issue was the police report didn't match the damage. The bumper was completely caved in on the passenger side, but he said the deer jumped from the left. It just didn't make sense. I actually saw a deer in my neighborhood the other night, which is weird because I live in an apartment complex right off the highway. It was just standing by the dumpsters, staring at me. I tried to take a picture with my phone, but the flash went off and it bolted into the trees. My phone camera is so annoying, I can never remember how to turn the flash off permanently. Every time there's an update, it resets all my settings. Apple really needs to fix that. \n\nBut yeah, blurting things out? No, I'm usually just trying to get a word in edgewise with these people.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often physically hyperactive or always on the go compared with other kids?\n\nPatient: No, I wasn't really like that as a kid either. I was always pretty quiet, just doing my own thing. Like I said, I'd just sit and stare at the wall or play with my Legos for hours. I loved Legos. I had this massive pirate ship set that took me like three days to build. It was the Black Seas Barracuda, I think? It had these real cloth sails and little cannons that actually fired these tiny plastic cannonballs. I ended up losing most of the cannonballs under the couch, though. My mom used to get so mad when she'd vacuum and hear them rattling up the hose. Our vacuum was this ancient Hoover that weighed like fifty pounds. It sounded like a jet engine taking off in the living room. I swear, they don't make appliances like they used to. My microwave broke last month, just completely stopped heating things up. The turntable spun, the light came on, but the food was ice cold. I looked up how to fix it on YouTube, but all the comments were like, \"Don't touch the capacitor or you'll electrocute yourself,\" so I just threw it out and bought a new one at Target. \n\nBut no, I wasn't running around all the time or anything like that when I was little. I was definitely one of the calmer kids.\n\nDoctor: Were you often physically hyperactive as a child?\n\nPatient: No, I think you just asked me that, didn't you? Like I was saying, I wasn't really hyper. I just sat around a lot. Actually, speaking of sitting around, my mom has this old photo album she brought out last Thanksgiving. It's one of those ones with the sticky pages and the clear plastic you peel back? Those things are terrible for preserving photos, by the way. The acid in the glue turns everything yellow. My aunt is a scrapbooking fanatic, and she gave my mom a whole lecture about archival-quality paper. My aunt has an entire room in her house just for scrapbooking. She calls it her \"craft sanctuary.\" It's full of all these little punches and stamps and expensive markers. I don't know how she affords it all, honestly. She works part-time at a florist, so it's not like she's rolling in dough. But I guess everyone needs a hobby. \n\nBut yeah, like I said, I was a pretty calm kid. Not hyperactive.\n\n[Last Question]\nAs a child, did you often interrupt or blurt things out before other people had finished speaking?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

No, I don't think so. I was pretty quiet, like I said. I was usually the one getting talked over, even back then. I'd overthink things before doing them, mostly. Like, if my friends were jumping off the high dive at the public pool... we used to go to the one over on 4th street. They actually closed that place down a few years ago because of some city budget thing, which really sucks because they had the best snack bar. They sold those Choco Tacos? Man, I heard they discontinued those recently, which is insane to me. They were the best ice cream treat hands down. I used to get one every time my dad gave me a couple bucks... \n\nWait, what did you ask? Oh, acting without thinking. Yeah, no, I was usually the kid standing at the edge of the diving board for like ten minutes just staring at the water until the lifeguard yelled at me to either jump or climb back down the ladder. So, no, I didn't really just do stuff out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Did you have these attention problems in more than one place, like both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, my mom was always getting on my case about my room being a total disaster zone. I could never keep it clean. She'd tell me to go clean it, and I'd go in there and find some old comic book under my bed and just start reading that instead. I had this huge collection of X-Men comics. The ones from the 90s, you know? With the Jim Lee art. Man, those were the best. I actually still have them in a box somewhere in my apartment. I should really get those graded, I think some of them might be worth a little bit of money now. I saw a guy on YouTube the other day who found a pristine copy of X-Men #1 at a garage sale for like fifty cents, which is just crazy... \n\nBut yeah, home, school... it was pretty much the same deal everywhere. Just kind of floating through, not really focusing on what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often restless or unable to stay seated?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wasn't really a squirmy kid. Like I said before, I was mostly just sitting there, zoning out. I remember in third grade, Mrs. Gable's class... she had this giant fish tank in the back of the room. It had these neon tetras and this one really aggressive angelfish that kept biting the others. I used to just sit at my desk and watch that angelfish for what felt like hours. I actually wanted to get a fish tank for my apartment recently, but my landlord is super strict about pets. Even fish, which is ridiculous. What's a fish going to do, ruin the carpet? I mean, I guess if the tank broke... water damage is no joke. I process insurance claims for a living, so I see water damage claims all the time. People leave their sinks running or a pipe bursts while they're on vacation in Florida... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Being restless. No, I could stay in my seat fine. I just wasn't really paying attention to the teacher while I was in it.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often interrupting other people or blurting out answers before a question was finished?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I was pretty quiet, honestly. Blurting things out wasn't really my style. I was usually too busy in my own head to even realize a question was being asked, let alone try to answer it first. My buddy Mark, though, he was terrible about that. We were in Boy Scouts together—Troop 412. We used to go camping up near Bear Lake every summer. The mosquitoes up there were absolutely brutal. I remember one year, I forgot my bug spray and I had like thirty bites just on my left leg. I tried putting calamine lotion on them, but that stuff never really works, does it? It just gets all crusty and pink and flakes off on your sheets. My mom was so mad because I got it all over my sleeping bag, and you can't just throw a down sleeping bag in the regular wash, you have to use those special tennis balls in the dryer... \n\nWait, sorry. Interrupting people. Yeah, no, I didn't really do that. I was more likely to not say anything at all.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often physically hyperactive, like feeling driven to move around or being unable to stay still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I never had that feeling of like, a motor running inside me or whatever. I was perfectly fine just sitting there, not moving much. Honestly, I've always been more of a low-energy kind of guy. Even now, my ideal weekend is just crashing on the couch and binge-watching something. I started rewatching Breaking Bad last week. It's crazy how different that show feels when you watch it a second time. Like, you pick up on all these little details they planted in the first season. I think Vince Gilligan is a genius. I tried to get my girlfriend to watch it with me, but she's not really into the violent stuff. She prefers those reality baking shows. We watched The Great British Baking Show, and I swear, every time we watch it I end up craving a croissant or a tart or something. I actually tried making sourdough bread during the pandemic, like everyone else did. My starter died after two weeks though. It smelled like old gym socks, which I guess means it went bad? I don't know, baking is too much of a science for me. You have to measure everything so precisely. If you're off by a little bit of flour, the whole thing is ruined. \n\nBut yeah, hyperactive? No. I was the kid who would just sit still and stare at the wall.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of being unable to sit still or being physically hyperactive as an adult, too?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I said, I've never been the hyper type. Even now at work, I sit at my desk for like eight hours a day processing claims and I'm fine just sitting there. Well, I mean, my back isn't fine. I actually had to buy one of those lumbar support pillows off Amazon because those office chairs are just terrible. You'd think a big insurance company could afford decent chairs for their employees, right? But no, it's all about the bottom line. My buddy Dave—he works in IT on the fourth floor—he actually managed to get them to buy him a standing desk. He claimed he had some ergonomic doctor's note or something. I tried standing at his desk once, and honestly, my feet started hurting after like twenty minutes. I don't get the hype. Plus, I feel like if I was standing all day, I'd just want to lean on something. I saw this thing online the other day, it's like a leaning stool? It looks so uncomfortable, like half a bicycle seat... \n\nAnyway, no, I don't feel driven to move around or anything like that. I'm pretty stationary.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently find yourself interrupting other people or blurting things out before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really do that either. I'm usually the one getting interrupted, honestly. I'll be in the middle of explaining a claim to a supervisor and they just talk right over me. It's super frustrating, especially when it's a complicated policy. We have this one underwriter, Sarah, and she is the absolute worst about it. She acts like she knows exactly what you're going to say before you even finish your sentence. And half the time she's wrong! Like last Tuesday, I was trying to tell her about this auto claim where the guy hit a deer, but the deer actually survived and ran off, so there was no carcass for proof. She kept cutting me off saying it was a comprehensive claim, which, yes, I know that, but the issue was the police report didn't match the damage. The bumper was completely caved in on the passenger side, but he said the deer jumped from the left. It just didn't make sense. I actually saw a deer in my neighborhood the other night, which is weird because I live in an apartment complex right off the highway. It was just standing by the dumpsters, staring at me. I tried to take a picture with my phone, but the flash went off and it bolted into the trees. My phone camera is so annoying, I can never remember how to turn the flash off permanently. Every time there's an update, it resets all my settings. Apple really needs to fix that. \n\nBut yeah, blurting things out? No, I'm usually just trying to get a word in edgewise with these people.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often physically hyperactive or always on the go compared with other kids?\n\nPatient: No, I wasn't really like that as a kid either. I was always pretty quiet, just doing my own thing. Like I said, I'd just sit and stare at the wall or play with my Legos for hours. I loved Legos. I had this massive pirate ship set that took me like three days to build. It was the Black Seas Barracuda, I think? It had these real cloth sails and little cannons that actually fired these tiny plastic cannonballs. I ended up losing most of the cannonballs under the couch, though. My mom used to get so mad when she'd vacuum and hear them rattling up the hose. Our vacuum was this ancient Hoover that weighed like fifty pounds. It sounded like a jet engine taking off in the living room. I swear, they don't make appliances like they used to. My microwave broke last month, just completely stopped heating things up. The turntable spun, the light came on, but the food was ice cold. I looked up how to fix it on YouTube, but all the comments were like, \"Don't touch the capacitor or you'll electrocute yourself,\" so I just threw it out and bought a new one at Target. \n\nBut no, I wasn't running around all the time or anything like that when I was little. I was definitely one of the calmer kids.\n\nDoctor: Were you often physically hyperactive as a child?\n\nPatient: No, I think you just asked me that, didn't you? Like I was saying, I wasn't really hyper. I just sat around a lot. Actually, speaking of sitting around, my mom has this old photo album she brought out last Thanksgiving. It's one of those ones with the sticky pages and the clear plastic you peel back? Those things are terrible for preserving photos, by the way. The acid in the glue turns everything yellow. My aunt is a scrapbooking fanatic, and she gave my mom a whole lecture about archival-quality paper. My aunt has an entire room in her house just for scrapbooking. She calls it her \"craft sanctuary.\" It's full of all these little punches and stamps and expensive markers. I don't know how she affords it all, honestly. She works part-time at a florist, so it's not like she's rolling in dough. But I guess everyone needs a hobby. \n\nBut yeah, like I said, I was a pretty calm kid. Not hyperactive.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often interrupt or blurt things out before other people had finished speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I was pretty quiet, like I said. And the air conditioning was broken, so we had to roll the windows down the whole way. It was August, so it was miserable. Yeah, definitely. Looking back, this isn't a new thing for me at all. Even in elementary school, my teachers used to write on my report cards that I was bright but I lost my worksheets all the time. I'd miss instructions, and they always had to remind me to actually finish my classwork. I remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she used to have this big red stamp that said "INCOMPLETE" and she'd stamp it on the top of my papers. She wore these really thick glasses with a chain on them so they'd hang around her neck. And she always smelled like peppermint. She kept a jar of those little red and white peppermints on her desk, the ones wrapped in clear plastic? Sometimes she'd give them out if you got a perfect score on a spelling test. I never really got them because I'd always rush through and misspell something stupid, like forgetting the second 'm' in 'accommodate' or whatever. Spelling bees were the absolute worst. We had to stand up in front of the whole class, and my palms would get so sweaty. I actually tripped over a chair leg once walking up to the front. 

But yeah, I've always struggled with the details and making those careless mistakes, even way back then.

They said I missed instructions a lot and always needed reminders to finish my classwork. Especially if I was drawing or building something. Like, I used to build these huge Lego sets. My uncle got me this massive pirate ship for my birthday one year, the one with the real cloth sails? Man, that took me forever to put together. We actually took it out to the creek behind our house to see if it would float, but it instantly tipped over and we lost like half the little plastic cannons in the mud. My mom was so mad because it was super expensive... \n\nBut yeah, I wasn't really a bouncy kid. I wasn't running around the classroom or anything like that. I was pretty quiet, just... staring out the window, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often act without thinking?\n\nPatient: Not really. I was actually kind of the opposite, I think. I'd overthink things before doing them, mostly. Like, if my friends were jumping off the high dive at the public pool... we used to go to the one over on 4th street. They actually closed that place down a few years ago because of some city budget thing, which really sucks because they had the best snack bar. They sold those Choco Tacos? Man, I heard they discontinued those recently, which is insane to me. They were the best ice cream treat hands down. I used to get one every time my dad gave me a couple bucks... \n\nWait, what did you ask? Oh, acting without thinking. Yeah, no, I was usually the kid standing at the edge of the diving board for like ten minutes just staring at the water until the lifeguard yelled at me to either jump or climb back down the ladder. So, no, I didn't really just do stuff out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Did you have these attention problems in more than one place, like both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, my mom was always getting on my case about my room being a total disaster zone. I could never keep it clean. She'd tell me to go clean it, and I'd go in there and find some old comic book under my bed and just start reading that instead. I had this huge collection of X-Men comics. The ones from the 90s, you know? With the Jim Lee art. Man, those were the best. I actually still have them in a box somewhere in my apartment. I should really get those graded, I think some of them might be worth a little bit of money now. I saw a guy on YouTube the other day who found a pristine copy of X-Men #1 at a garage sale for like fifty cents, which is just crazy... \n\nBut yeah, home, school... it was pretty much the same deal everywhere. Just kind of floating through, not really focusing on what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often restless or unable to stay seated?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wasn't really a squirmy kid. Like I said before, I was mostly just sitting there, zoning out. I remember in third grade, Mrs. Gable's class... she had this giant fish tank in the back of the room. It had these neon tetras and this one really aggressive angelfish that kept biting the others. I used to just sit at my desk and watch that angelfish for what felt like hours. I actually wanted to get a fish tank for my apartment recently, but my landlord is super strict about pets. Even fish, which is ridiculous. What's a fish going to do, ruin the carpet? I mean, I guess if the tank broke... water damage is no joke. I process insurance claims for a living, so I see water damage claims all the time. People leave their sinks running or a pipe bursts while they're on vacation in Florida... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Being restless. No, I could stay in my seat fine. I just wasn't really paying attention to the teacher while I was in it.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often interrupting other people or blurting out answers before a question was finished?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I was pretty quiet, honestly. Blurting things out wasn't really my style. I was usually too busy in my own head to even realize a question was being asked, let alone try to answer it first. My buddy Mark, though, he was terrible about that. We were in Boy Scouts together—Troop 412. We used to go camping up near Bear Lake every summer. The mosquitoes up there were absolutely brutal. I remember one year, I forgot my bug spray and I had like thirty bites just on my left leg. I tried putting calamine lotion on them, but that stuff never really works, does it? It just gets all crusty and pink and flakes off on your sheets. My mom was so mad because I got it all over my sleeping bag, and you can't just throw a down sleeping bag in the regular wash, you have to use those special tennis balls in the dryer... \n\nWait, sorry. Interrupting people. Yeah, no, I didn't really do that. I was more likely to not say anything at all.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often physically hyperactive, like feeling driven to move around or being unable to stay still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I never had that feeling of like, a motor running inside me or whatever. I was perfectly fine just sitting there, not moving much. Honestly, I've always been more of a low-energy kind of guy. Even now, my ideal weekend is just crashing on the couch and binge-watching something. I started rewatching Breaking Bad last week. It's crazy how different that show feels when you watch it a second time. Like, you pick up on all these little details they planted in the first season. I think Vince Gilligan is a genius. I tried to get my girlfriend to watch it with me, but she's not really into the violent stuff. She prefers those reality baking shows. We watched The Great British Baking Show, and I swear, every time we watch it I end up craving a croissant or a tart or something. I actually tried making sourdough bread during the pandemic, like everyone else did. My starter died after two weeks though. It smelled like old gym socks, which I guess means it went bad? I don't know, baking is too much of a science for me. You have to measure everything so precisely. If you're off by a little bit of flour, the whole thing is ruined. \n\nBut yeah, hyperactive? No. I was the kid who would just sit still and stare at the wall.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of being unable to sit still or being physically hyperactive as an adult, too?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I said, I've never been the hyper type. Even now at work, I sit at my desk for like eight hours a day processing claims and I'm fine just sitting there. Well, I mean, my back isn't fine. I actually had to buy one of those lumbar support pillows off Amazon because those office chairs are just terrible. You'd think a big insurance company could afford decent chairs for their employees, right? But no, it's all about the bottom line. My buddy Dave—he works in IT on the fourth floor—he actually managed to get them to buy him a standing desk. He claimed he had some ergonomic doctor's note or something. I tried standing at his desk once, and honestly, my feet started hurting after like twenty minutes. I don't get the hype. Plus, I feel like if I was standing all day, I'd just want to lean on something. I saw this thing online the other day, it's like a leaning stool? It looks so uncomfortable, like half a bicycle seat... \n\nAnyway, no, I don't feel driven to move around or anything like that. I'm pretty stationary.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently find yourself interrupting other people or blurting things out before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really do that either. I'm usually the one getting interrupted, honestly. I'll be in the middle of explaining a claim to a supervisor and they just talk right over me. It's super frustrating, especially when it's a complicated policy. We have this one underwriter, Sarah, and she is the absolute worst about it. She acts like she knows exactly what you're going to say before you even finish your sentence. And half the time she's wrong! Like last Tuesday, I was trying to tell her about this auto claim where the guy hit a deer, but the deer actually survived and ran off, so there was no carcass for proof. She kept cutting me off saying it was a comprehensive claim, which, yes, I know that, but the issue was the police report didn't match the damage. The bumper was completely caved in on the passenger side, but he said the deer jumped from the left. It just didn't make sense. I actually saw a deer in my neighborhood the other night, which is weird because I live in an apartment complex right off the highway. It was just standing by the dumpsters, staring at me. I tried to take a picture with my phone, but the flash went off and it bolted into the trees. My phone camera is so annoying, I can never remember how to turn the flash off permanently. Every time there's an update, it resets all my settings. Apple really needs to fix that. \n\nBut yeah, blurting things out? No, I'm usually just trying to get a word in edgewise with these people.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often physically hyperactive or always on the go compared with other kids?\n\nPatient: No, I wasn't really like that as a kid either. I was always pretty quiet, just doing my own thing. Like I said, I'd just sit and stare at the wall or play with my Legos for hours. I loved Legos. I had this massive pirate ship set that took me like three days to build. It was the Black Seas Barracuda, I think? It had these real cloth sails and little cannons that actually fired these tiny plastic cannonballs. I ended up losing most of the cannonballs under the couch, though. My mom used to get so mad when she'd vacuum and hear them rattling up the hose. Our vacuum was this ancient Hoover that weighed like fifty pounds. It sounded like a jet engine taking off in the living room. I swear, they don't make appliances like they used to. My microwave broke last month, just completely stopped heating things up. The turntable spun, the light came on, but the food was ice cold. I looked up how to fix it on YouTube, but all the comments were like, \"Don't touch the capacitor or you'll electrocute yourself,\" so I just threw it out and bought a new one at Target. \n\nBut no, I wasn't running around all the time or anything like that when I was little. I was definitely one of the calmer kids.\n\nDoctor: Were you often physically hyperactive as a child?\n\nPatient: No, I think you just asked me that, didn't you? Like I was saying, I wasn't really hyper. I just sat around a lot. Actually, speaking of sitting around, my mom has this old photo album she brought out last Thanksgiving. It's one of those ones with the sticky pages and the clear plastic you peel back? Those things are terrible for preserving photos, by the way. The acid in the glue turns everything yellow. My aunt is a scrapbooking fanatic, and she gave my mom a whole lecture about archival-quality paper. My aunt has an entire room in her house just for scrapbooking. She calls it her \"craft sanctuary.\" It's full of all these little punches and stamps and expensive markers. I don't know how she affords it all, honestly. She works part-time at a florist, so it's not like she's rolling in dough. But I guess everyone needs a hobby. \n\nBut yeah, like I said, I was a pretty calm kid. Not hyperactive.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often interrupt or blurt things out before other people had finished speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I was pretty quiet, like I said. I was usually the one getting talked over, even back then. My older brother was so loud, he just dominated every conversation at the dinner table. He was always talking about baseball. He was obsessed with the Atlanta Braves. He had all these stats memorized, like ERAs and batting averages from guys in the 90s. We actually went to a game once at Turner Field before they tore it down. We drove all the way down to Georgia in my dad's old station wagon. It had that fake wood paneling on the side? And the air conditioning was broken, so we had to roll the windows down the whole way. It was August, so it was miserable. We stopped at this diner off I-75 and I got a milkshake, but they used that cheap artificial strawberry syrup, you know the kind that tastes like medicine? I couldn't even finish it. \n\nBut yeah, no, I wasn't really the type to blurt things out or interrupt. I just kind of listened.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you already have trouble paying close attention to details or make careless mistakes in schoolwork?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Looking back, this isn't a new thing for me at all. Even in elementary school, my teachers used to write on my report cards that I was bright but I lost my worksheets all the time. I'd miss instructions, and they always had to remind me to actually finish my classwork. I remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she used to have this big red stamp that said \"INCOMPLETE\" and she'd stamp it on the top of my papers. She wore these really thick glasses with a chain on them so they'd hang around her neck. And she always smelled like peppermint. She kept a jar of those little red and white peppermints on her desk, the ones wrapped in clear plastic? Sometimes she'd give them out if you got a perfect score on a spelling test. I never really got them because I'd always rush through and misspell something stupid, like forgetting the second 'm' in 'accommodate' or whatever. Spelling bees were the absolute worst. We had to stand up in front of the whole class, and my palms would get so sweaty. I actually tripped over a chair leg once walking up to the front. \n\nBut yeah, I've always struggled with the details and making those careless mistakes, even way back then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. We had to stand up in front of the whole class, and my palms would get so sweaty. I actually tripped over a chair leg once walking up to the front. \n\nBut yeah, I've always struggled with the details and making those careless mistakes, even way back then."
